## Page 1


FOURIER RESTRICTION NORM METHOD ADAPTED TO
CONTROLLED PATHS: STOCHASTIC WAVE EQUATIONS
ANDREIA CHAPOUTO, JIAWEI LI, AND TADAHIRO OH
Abstract. We investigate the pathwise well-posedness issue of the stochastic nonlinear
wave equation (SNLW) with a multiplicative noise. While the Ito solution theory (= random
field solution theory) was established in the ’80s, its pathwise well-posedness has remained a
challenging open problem for over forty years. By building a unified framework for the Fourier
restriction norm method adapted to the U p- and V p-spaces, due to Koch and Tataru (2007),
and the Young / rough integration theory via the sewing lemma and controlled paths due
to Gubinelli (2004) along with the random tensor estimate for multiple stochastic integrals
with respect to (fractional) Brownian motions, we establish pathwise local well-posedness
of SNLW in optimal regularity ranges. In particular, in the one-dimensional case with a
white-in-time noise, our result covers the case of an almost space-time white noise, which is
optimal within the framework of one-parameter rough paths.

2, the family {Bn}n∈Zd corresponds to a family of independent Brownian
motions, satisfying (1.7), and the noise ζ in (1.1) reduces to a space-time white noise ξ. In
this case, since the work [167], there have been intensive research activities on SNLW with
(more general) multiplicative noises from the viewpoint of the Ito solution theory and the
random field solution theory; see, for example, [30, 29, 115, 119, 51, 116, 49, 149, 114, 20, 52,
146, 147, 5, 21, 120, 56, 117, 23, 92]. See also [50, 155, 48, 53] for further references therein.
See [3, 4, 107, 40] for results in the fractional-in-time case. Here, the Ito solution theory
and the random field solution theory refer to approaches where the stochastic convolution
Ψ(u) in (1.5) (possibly with u replaced by a more general function F(u)) is constructed as a
(suitable) limit in L2(Ω) via stochastic calculus. We also point out that the study on (1.1)
goes further back in the literature due to its direct connection to stochastic integration with
respect to a multi-parameter white noise; see, for example, [26, 169, 27, 28, 89, 65, 122].
Our main interest in this paper is to study the question of pathwise well-posedness of
SNLW (1.1). Informally, we say that SNLW (1.1) is pathwise locally well-posed (given (ϕ0, ϕ1)
and Φ) if there exists a set Σ ⊂Ωwith P(Σ) = 1 such that, for each ω ∈Σ, there exists a
solution uω to the Duhamel formulation (1.3) on a time interval [0, T] for some almost surely
positive stopping time T, where the stochastic convolution Ψω(uω) in (1.5) is constructed in
the pathwise manner (namely, using information of W β,ω in (1.6) and the unknown uω for
this particular ω ∈Σ, not as a limit in L2(Ω)). Here, we intentionally remained vague on
relevant function spaces, etc.; see Subsection 1.3 for a further discussion. The difficulty of
pathwise well-posedness theory as compared to the Ito solution theory can be seen even at

X ⊂C([0, 1]) such that (i) sample paths of a Brownian motion belong to X, almost surely,
and (ii) the bilinear operator I in (1.10), a priori defined for smooth functions, extends to a
continuous map on X × X into C([0, 1]); see [108] and [69, Proposition 1.1]. This shows that
when g = B, (1.9) is ill-posed in the pathwise sense, if we were to take only f(0) and B as
given data. See the next subsection for a further discussion. We point out that one important
consequence of the pathwise solution theory is that it allows for almost sure convergence of
various approximation schemes such as mollification, piecewise linear approximation, and
many other approximations. See, for example, [72], [71, Sections 10.3, 12.2, 13.3, and 17.7],
and [69, Sections 8.7 and 9.2].
Regarding pathwise well-posedness of SNLW with multiplicative noises, there are very
few results in the literature (see, for example, [153, 39]), limited to the fractional-in-time
case with the Hurst parameter β > 1
2 (namely, the so-called Young case). In particular,
pathwise well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) with a multiplicative white-in-time noise has remained
a challenging open problem for the last forty years since the work [167]. In this paper, we
establish the first pathwise local well-posedness result for SNLW (1.1) with a multiplicative
white-in-time noise (Theorem 1.5), as well as a new general pathwise local well-posedness
result in the fractional-in-time case (Theorem 1.4), thus answering the open question of forty
years. Our approach is based on building a unified framework for
(i) the Fourier restriction norm method, independently introduced by Bourgain [10] and
by Klainerman and Machedon [97] and further developed by Koch and Tataru [101]
in the setting of the V p-spaces of functions of bounded p-variation and their preduals
(the so-called Up-spaces), and
(ii) the Young / rough integration theory via the sewing lemma and controlled paths due
to Gubinelli [76], subsequent to a seminal work [109] by Lyons on rough path theory,
where the random tensor estimate for multiple stochastic integrals with respect to (fractional)
Brownian motions (Lemma 2.12) plays a crucial role in (ii). We note that our pathwise
well-posedness results are essentially sharp in terms of spatial regularity. See Remark 1.7 (i).
Furthermore, in the one-dimensional case with a white-in-time noise, our result covers the case
of an almost space-time white noise, which is optimal within the framework of one-parameter
rough paths; see Remark 1.10.

at the zeroth spatial frequency. For this reason, we choose to work with the Klein-Gordon
version (1.1) in this paper, and we simply refer to it as the stochastic nonlinear wave equation.
Our main results (Theorems 1.4 and 1.5) are on pathwise local well-posedness of SNLW (1.1).
As such, the defocusing / focusing nature of the equation does not play any role in our analysis,
and thus we simply work with the “focusing” case (namely, with −uk in (1.1)) in the following.
1.2. Overview. The study of random dispersive PDEs, broadly interpreted with random
initial data and / or stochastic forcing, was initiated in seminal works [11, 12] by Bourgain
in the context of invariant Gibbs measures for nonlinear Schr¨odinger equations (NLS). This
study lying at the intersection of dispersive PDEs and stochastic analysis has attracted
much attention and has been studied intensively; see, for example, [24, 127, 46, 25, 6,
152, 79, 139, 141, 81, 13, 129, 134, 15, 130, 161, 140, 68, 171, 104, 106]. See also surveys
[7, 163]. In particular, over the last five years, we have witnessed several breakthrough results
[80, 58, 59, 16], introducing novel tools and ideas such as paracontrolled calculus in the
dispersive setting, random averaging operators, and the theory of random tensors. We point
out that many works in the literature focused on probabilistic well-posedness with random
initial data and / or additive noises, where the difficulty comes from spatial roughness in
making sense of products and nonlinearities. In contrast, the main difficulty of the pathwise
well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) with a multiplicative noise comes from temporal roughness in
making sense of the stochastic convolution Ψ(u) in (1.5) in the pathwise manner.
Focusing only on temporal regularity, let us revisit the integral I = I(f, g) in (1.10):

In the setting of H¨older continuous functions, the integral I = I(f, g) in (1.11) can be
understood as the so-called Young integral [170] if f ∈Cα1 and g ∈Cα2 with α1 + α2 > 1.

When α1 = α2 = α, this condition reduces to α > 1
2. In a seminal work [109], Lyons introduced
the theory of rough paths, which allows us to interpret the integral I = I(f, g) in (1.11) as a
rough integral when 0 < α ≤1
2. In a subsequent breakthrough work [76], Gubinelli introduced
the notion of controlled paths, which provided a convenient framework to make sense of a
rough integral I = I(f, g) via the so-called sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4).3 The theory of rough
paths has been applied widely in the study of pathwise well-posedness of stochastic differential
equations driven by rough noises such as a Brownian motion and pathwise well-posedness of
stochastic parabolic PDEs; see, for example, [82, 31, 83, 32, 86, 64, 88, 148, 91]. See also the
monographs [111, 71, 69] for further discussions on the subject. We also point out that the
theory of rough paths has led to the development of the theory of regularity structures by
Hairer [87] and paracontrolled distributions by Gubinelli, Imkeller, and Perkowski [78].

The stochastic convolution Ψ(u) in (1.5) comes with the linear wave propagator S(t −t′)
as compared to (1.14). In the usual study of dispersive PDEs, dispersion induces oscillations
providing certain cancellation (say, via integration by parts). However, the integrator dW β(t′)
in (1.5) is of negative regularity, and as a result, the linear wave propagator S(t −t′) in (1.5)
is an enemy in our problem, preventing tools such as integration by parts. We circumvent
this issue by working with the so-called interaction representation (see (1.27)) in the vectorial
formulation, and construct the stochastic convolution Ψ(u) (at the level of the interaction
representation) as a Young / rough integral, depending on the value of the Hurst parameter β.
In order to handle the nonlinear Duhamel integral term (= the third term on the right-hand
side of (1.3)) for d ≥2, we make use of dispersion in the form of the Strichartz estimates
(Lemma 5.5). However, the usual Strichartz spaces (not capturing temporal regularity) or
the Xs,b-spaces in the Fourier restriction norm method are not compatible with the pathwise
construction of the stochastic convolution Ψ(u) as a Young / rough integral. We build a
unified framework for treating both the nonlinear Duhamel integral term and the stochastic
convolution Ψ(u) by working with the refined Fourier restriction norm method adapted to the
Up- and V p-spaces, introduced by Koch and Tataru [101]; see Subsection 2.3 for the definitions
and basic properties of these function spaces. In the rough case (β = 1
2), we reduce (1.3)

Proposition 1.2. Let d ≥1. Given an integer k ≥2, let scrit be as in (1.18) when d ≥2
and (1.20) when d = 1. Suppose that one of the following conditions holds:
(i) s > scrit,
when (d, k) ∈{(2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 2)}, or
(ii) s ≥scrit,
otherwise.
(1.21)
Then, NLW (1.15) is locally well-posed in Hs(Td) := Hs(Td) × Hs−1(Td).
Proposition 1.2 follows from a standard contraction argument, using Sobolev’s inequality
for d = 1 and the Strichartz estimates (Lemma 5.5) for d ≥2. See the proof of Proposition A.1
on local well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) in the Ito sense, where we establish the nonlinear
estimates. In Section 5, we revisit the nonlinear estimates necessary for proving Proposition 1.2
in the context of the Fourier restriction norm method adapted to the Up- and V p-spaces; see
Subsection 2.3 for the definitions of these spaces.
Remark 1.3. In the focusing case (with −uk in (1.15)), Proposition 1.2 is essentially sharp;4
see [105, 45, 131, 67] for the known ill-posedness results. See also [162, Exercise 3.64 and
3.67].
In the defocusing case (with +uk in (1.15)), the question of local well-posedness
of NLW (1.15) still remains open in the range (i) max(sscaling, 0) ≤s ≤max(sconf, 0) for
(d, k) ∈{(2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 2)} and (ii) sscaling ≤s < sconf for (d, k) ∈{(2, 4), (4, 2)}.

In the following, we discuss our strategy and explain a meaning of pathwise solutions
constructed in Theorem 1.4. A natural attempt would be to try to construct the stochastic
convolution Ψ(u) in (1.5) as a Young integral, for which we need to make use of the temporal
regularity of the unknown u in (1.3).
However, due to the presence of the linear wave
propagator S(t) in (1.3), we expect positive temporal regularity for u only at the expense of
spatial regularity. To bypass this issue, we will instead work with the so-called interaction
representation, which is morally given by “S(t)−1u(t)”; see (1.27) for a precise definition.
In order to introduce this change of unknowns, we work with the following vectorial
formulation of (1.3) for a vector ⃗u = (u, v) with v = ∂tu:

We overcome this issue by employing the refined Fourier restriction norm method adapted to
the V p-spaces of functions of bounded p-variation and their preduals (the so-called Up-spaces),
introduced by Koch and Tataru with their collaborators [100, 101, 85, 90]. See Subsection 2.3
for the definitions and basic properties of these spaces. Moreover, these spaces enjoy the
following properties:
(i) The Up- and V p-spaces scale like the L∞-space, and thus the Up

deterministic analysis on the nonlinear term ⃗N(⃗u) in (1.35). We note that the Up-V p′
duality plays a crucial role in (the proof of) the linear estimate (2.19) of Lemma 2.6.
(ii) The V p-spaces are also adapted to the construction of Young (and rough) integrals;
see [71, 63]. In Section 3, we present a review on the Young / rough integration theory
for functions of bounded p-variation, based on the sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4), in
particular for readers in dispersive PDEs who may not be familiar with the subject.
We pay particular attention to make notations and spaces introduced in Subsection 2.3
(for PDE analysis) and Section 3 (for rough analysis) compatible.

1.4. Remarks & comments. In this subsection, we state remarks and comments.
Remark 1.6. (i) The framework introduced in this paper is general and can be adapted
to study pathwise well-posedness of other stochastic dispersive PDEs with multiplicative
noises. See [35, 36]. In the case of SNLW (1.1), the smoothing coming from the linear
wave propagator S(t) in (1.4) in fact plays a crucial role in establishing (1.34) and (1.38) on
the random drivers ⃗X and ⃗X; see Propositions 4.1 and 4.2. For the stochastic NLS with a
multiplicative white-in-time noise, the bounds (1.34) and (1.38) do not hold for the associated
first order and second order drivers X and X. Such bounds hold only with a slight loss in
spatial regularity. Namely, temporal roughness induces a loss in spatial regularity, causing a
substantial difficulty as compared to SNLW studied in this paper. We will address this issue
in forthcoming works [37, 38].
(ii) In [83], Gubinelli and Tindel developed the convolution Young / rough integration theory
and proved pathwise local well-posedness of the stochastic heat equation (SHE) with a
multiplicative noise, where the operator norms of the random drivers were estimated by
studying their Hilbert-Schmidt norms. In [157], the second and third authors with Y. Shao
applied the random tensor estimate (Lemma 2.12) for multiple stochastic integrals with respect
to fractional Brownian motions to study pathwise local well-posedness of SHE and improved
the results in [83] in both the Young and rough cases. See [157] for a further discussion.
(iii) In the study of dispersive PDEs, the sewing lemma was used in several contexts. In [77],
Gubinelli developed nonlinear rough paths (see also [83, 64, 126]) and used the sewing lemma,
as a replacement of the Fourier restriction norm method, to study local well-posedness of the
deterministic KdV equation. See also [136]. In [42, 44, 75], the authors studied well-posedness
issues of modulated dispersive PDEs by constructing the nonlinear Duhamel integral terms as
a nonlinear Young integral via the sewing lemma.
Remark 1.7. (i) In view of Remark 1.3 on the deterministic NLW (1.15), the spatial regularity
condition (1.21) imposed in Theorems 1.4 and 1.5 is essentially sharp. See also Remark 1.10.


## Page 15


FOURIER RESTRICTION NORM METHOD ADAPTED TO CONTROLLED PATHS
15
(ii) In this paper, we considered SNLW (1.1) with an integer k ≥2 for simplicity of presentation.
It is possible to adapt our analysis to the nonlinearity of the form |u|k−1u for a non-integer
value of k > 1. In doing so, one needs to impose the compatibility condition k > ⌊s⌋+ 1
when k is not an odd integer to ensure smoothness of the nonlinearity. See [45] for a further
discussion.
(iii) While we chose to work with a noise of the form uΦζ in this paper, our approach also
extends to treat a more general noise of the form F(u)Φζ for a bounded continuous function F
with a suitable regularity. A required modification is straightforward, and thus we omit
details.
It is of interest to study pathwise local well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) with a noise of the
form uℓΦζ for an integer ℓ≥2. In the white-in-time case, one needs to employ nonlinear
rough path theory developed in [77, 83]; see also [136]. We mention a recent work [36], where
we studied pathwise local well-posedness of the stochastic KdV equation with a bilinear noise
u2Φζ by making use of multilinear dispersion on the noise term.
Remark 1.8. (i) In the rough case (= white-in-time case with β = 1
2), we introduced the
second order driver ⃗X in (1.37). In view of (4.7) with (4.4), each component of the matrix-
valued operator ⃗X is given by an iterated stochastic integral. In this paper, we interpreted
it as an iterated Wiener-Ito integral. In Appendix A, we show that our pathwise solutions
constructed in Theorem 1.5 agree with Ito solutions to SNLW (1.1), where we interpret the
stochastic convolution Ψ(u) in (1.5) as an Ito integral; see Proposition A.5. Theorem 1.5 also
holds if we interpret the pair (⃗X, ⃗X) as a geometric rough path; see [69, Section 2.2]. In this
case, pathwise solutions agree with Stratonovich solutions to SNLW (1.1), where we interpret
the equation (1.1) in the Stratonovich sense. See [69, Chapter 5] for a further discussion.
(ii) Let β = 1
2. In the defocusing case (with +uk, k ∈2N + 1, in (1.1)), in view of the
agreement with Ito solutions, pathwise local-in-time solutions to SNLW (1.1) constructed in
Theorem 1.5 with s = 1 can be extended globally in time in the energy (sub)critical regime
(namely, when sscaling ≤1, where sscaling is as in (1.16)). While it is not explicitly written in
the literature, one can adapt the argument by de Bouard and Debussche [54, 55], developed
in the context of stochastic NLS, and establish an a priori H1-bound on Ito solutions to
SNLW (1.1) via the energy conservation for the deterministic NLW (1.15), Ito’s lemma, and
the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality. In the energy-subcritical case (sscaling < 1), this
allows us to conclude global well-posedness in H1(Td). One may also adapt the argument
in [41] via the I-method in the stochastic setting to extend global well-posedness to some
s < 1. See [1] for the energy-critical case (sscaling = 1). In the energy-supercritical case
(sscaling > 1), we do not expect global well-posedness in general in view of a recent work [158]
on the energy-supercritical defocusing NLW.
It is of interest to investigate the issue of global well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) (with the
defocusing nonlinearity +uk, k ∈2N + 1) in the Young case.
Remark 1.9. (i) In the rough case, the decomposition (1.42) is needed to treat the case (1.41).
In other situations, Theorem 1.5 follows from directly working on ⃗u (and its Gubinelli derivative
⃗u′) and studying the RDE (1.40) coupled with the controlled structure (1.39). For simplicity
of presentation, however, we present a unified approach and work on the RDE-PDE system
(1.43)-(1.44).

(ii) Let us mention and compare several works, where a decomposition of an unknown into
two unknowns as in (1.42) plays a crucial role. In [80], paracontrolled calculus, originally
introduced for stochastic parabolic PDEs [78, 33, 118], was adapted to study SNLW with an
additive noise. After the second order expansion via the explicit stochastic terms (see [80,
(1.13)]), the remainder part v was decomposed into v = X + Y , where X is rougher-in-space
but structured, and Y is smoother-in-space; see [80, (1.25) and (1.26)]. In this work, the fact
that the X-equation has a structure played an important role in deriving the final system
[80, (1.36)]. See [129, 15, 130, 16] for subsequent works in this direction. In [135], the authors
studied the hyperbolic Liouville equation (SNLW with an additive space-time white noise and
an exponential nonlinearity), and the remainder part v (after the first order expansion as
in [113, 12, 47]) was decomposed into v = X + Y , where, once again, X is rougher-in-space
but structured, and Y is smoother-in-space; see [135, (1.54) and (1.56)]. In this work, the
sign-definite structure of the rougher part X played a crucial role. We also mention the
work [57], where the (deterministic) derivative NLS was studied (with deterministic initial
data). In this work, the equation for the (gauged) unknown v was decomposed into a system
for v and the additional part w where w is smoother in time; see [57, (4.16) and (4.17)]. See
also [34].
In the current paper, the reduction of (1.29) to the RDE-PDE system (1.43)-(1.44) was done
in two steps. In the first step, we imposed the controlled structure (1.39) and reduced (1.29)
into a system (1.40) and (1.39) for two unknowns ⃗u and ⃗u′ (or we can view this as a system
for two unknowns ⃗u′ and ⃗R⃗X,⃗u, where the latter is smoother in time). We note that this first
step can be replaced by a paracontrolled ansatz (in time). In the second step, we applied the
decomposition (1.42) and arrived at the RDE-PDE system (1.43)-(1.44) for three unknowns
⃗v, ⃗w, and ⃗u′. One notable difference with the works [80, 135, 57] is that ⃗v is rougher in time
but smoother in space, while ⃗w is smoother in time but rougher in space. Namely, each part
has a (small) gain of regularity in one of the temporal or spatial direction.
Remark 1.10. Note that the condition (1.24) implies σ > −1
2. In particular, when d = 1,
Theorem 1.5 in the rough case (with β = 1
2) covers the case of an almost space-time white
noise (for example, when Φ = ⟨∂x⟩−ε for any ε > 0). We note that this is optimal within the
framework of one-parameter rough paths.
Let us briefly describe the main difficulty of SNLW with a multiplicative space-time white
noise, posed on the one-dimensional torus T:
∂2

x)u ± uk = uξ.
(1.45)
See [167, 128] for the Ito solution theory. See also Remark A.3. The space-time white noise
ξ has regularity −1
2 −ε in both the temporal and spatial directions, from which we expect
that the unknown has regularity 1
2 −ε in both the temporal and spatial directions (which is
precisely the regularity of the stochastic convolution Ψ(u) in (1.5) with u ≡1). Namely, we
have the deficiency of regularities in both the temporal and spatial directions, and thus the
usual one-parameter rough path analysis is not sufficient. See [43], where a partial progress
was made in the bi-parameter setting. We will address this issue in a forthcoming work. We
mention a recent work [17], where such bi-parameter analysis was developed in the study of
the one-dimensional wave maps equation. See also [92] for a further discussion on (1.45).

Remark 1.11. In this paper, we only consider β = 1
2 as the rough case. As long as we can
extend the bounds (1.34) and (1.38) to lower values of α (with α = β −ε for any small ε > 0),
the rough integration theory based on the second order rough path10 (⃗X, ⃗X) in Subsection 3.3
allows us to construct the rough integral I ⃗X,⃗X(⃗u) in the pathwise manner, provided that
β > 1
3. We note that as β decreases, higher spatial regularity is needed for pathwise local
well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) (in order to handle the nonlinear term in (1.35)). We will
address this issue in a future work. Lastly, we mention the works [60, 61, 62] on SNLW, forced

2.
1.5. Organization. In Section 2, we introduce basic notations and function spaces, including
spaces for multi-parameter functions (Subsection 2.2) and the Up- and V p-spaces (Subsec-
tion 2.3). We then provide a brief review on multiple stochastic integrals with respect to
fractional Brownian motions (Subsection 2.4) and establish the random tensor estimate
(Lemma 2.12) in this setting. We also recall Kolmogorov’s continuity criterion for rough paths
(Lemma 2.13). In Section 3, we go over Young / rough integration theory for functions of
bounded p-variation, based on the sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4). The materials presented in
Section 3 may be standard by now, but we decided to include them in an accessible manner
for readers in dispersive PDEs who may not be familiar with the subject. In Section 4, we
study regularity properties of the random operators ⃗X and ⃗X, defined in (1.32) and (1.37),
respectively, using the random tensor estimate. In Section 5, we establish (deterministic)
nonlinear estimates in the Up-setting, in particular using the Strichartz estimates for d ≥2.
In Section 6, by reducing (1.29) to the YDE (6.1), we prove pathwise local well-posedness
of SNLW (1.1) in the Young case (Theorem 1.4). In Section 7, by reducing (1.29) to the
RDE-PDE system (1.43)-(1.44), we prove pathwise local well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) in
the rough case (Theorem 1.5). In Appendix A, after going over the Ito solution theory (see
Proposition A.1 and Remark A.3), we show that, in the white-in-time case (= rough case),
our pathwise solutions agree with Ito solutions. In Appendix B, we provide a brief discussion
on an optimizer for the local-in-time V p-norm defined in (2.15). In Appendix C, we present a
proof of the sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4) adapted to our setting. In Appendix D, we go over
the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality on Banach spaces, which we used in Lemma A.2 on
the construction of the stochastic convolution as an Ito integral.

2.3. Up- and V p-spaces and related estimates. In this subsection, we review the defini-
tions and basic properties of the Up- and V p-spaces, developed by Tataru, Koch, and their
collaborators [100, 101, 85, 90] in the context of dispersive PDEs. The V p-space denotes the
space of functions of bounded p-variation, whose introduction dates back to the classical work
of Wiener [168], and whose restriction to continuous functions is often denoted by Cp−var in
the rough path literature; see, for example, [71]. The Up-space, introduced in [100, 101], is the
predual of V p′, where p′ denotes the H¨older conjugate of p. We refer readers to [85, 90, 99, 102]
for detailed proofs of the relevant properties of these function spaces.

2.4. Fractional Brownian motion and multiple stochastic integrals. In this subsection,
we recall the definition and basic properties of fractional Brownian motions and of Wiener
integrals with respect to fractional Brownian motions. See [125, Chapter 5] for further details.
See also [9, 124].
We first recall the definition of a fractional Brownian motion, introduced in [103] and
further developed in [112].
Definition 2.7. Let 0 < β < 1. A (real-valued) fractional Brownian motion {B(t)}t∈R+ with
the Hurst parameter β is a centered Gaussian process with covariance given by
E[B(t1)B(t2)] = 1

We now state the random tensor estimate for multiple stochastic integrals, which plays a
crucial role in studying the mapping properties of the random operators ⃗X and ⃗X in (1.32)
and (1.37), respectively, in Section 4. The random tensor estimate was first introduced in a
seminal work [59] by Deng, Nahmod, and Yue in the context of random variables; see also
[15, 143]. See [12] for a precursor of the random tensor estimate. In [144], it was extended to
the case of multiple Wiener-Ito integrals (with the Hurst parameter β = 1
2). Here, we further
extend this result to treat multiple stochastic integrals with respect to fractional Brownian
motions with Hurst parameter 1
2 < β < 1, by closely following the argument in [59, 144].

2.6. Kolmogorov’s continuity criterion. We conclude the section by recalling a version
of Kolmogorov’s continuity criterion for a pair of two-parameter operator-valued stochastic
processes (X, X) satisfying Chen’s relation:
(δX)t1,t2,t3 = 0,
(δX)t1,t2,t3 = Xt1,t2 ◦Xt2,t3
(2.49)
for t1 ≥t2 ≥t3 ≥0. See Definition 3.6 (i). Since a straightforward modification of the proof of
[69, Theorem 3.1] yields the following lemma, we omit its proof. Furthermore, as pointed out

3. Young and rough integration
In this section, we provide a review on the construction of (linear) Young and rough integrals,
based on the sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4) and the theory of controlled paths in [76, 83]; see
also [66, 69]. While the Young and rough integration theory developed in [76, 83] was based
on H¨older continuous functions, in view of our application to dispersive PDEs, we will instead
go over the Young / rough integration theory for functions of bounded p-variation, which is
compatible with the Fourier restriction norm method adapted to the Up- and V p-spaces (see
Subsection 2.3). The materials presented in this section may be standard by now, but we
decided to include them in an accessible manner for readers in dispersive PDEs who may not
be familiar with the Young / rough integration theory.
In Subsection 3.1, we recall the notion of controls and state a version of the sewing lemma
adapted to the V p-spaces. In Subsection 3.2, we go over the construction of Young integrals
via the sewing lemma. In Subsection 3.3, by introducing the notion of controlled paths in
the p-variation setting (Definition 3.6), we go over the construction of rough integrals for
controlled paths.

3.1. Sewing lemma. We first recall the notion of a regular control function and a suitable
generalization of bounded p-variation functions in the n-parameter setting, n = 2, 3, adapted
to our setting. See, for example, [63, Subsection 2.1] for a presentation close to our setting.
See also [109, 111, 110, 99] and [71, Subsection 1.2.1 and 5.1.1] for a further discussion.

We now present a version of the sewing lemma adapted to the current p-variation setting.
See, for example, [63, Lemma 2.2] for an analogous statement whose proof is based on an
adaptation of the proof of [69, Lemma 4.2] in the H¨older regularity setting. Our statement
corresponds to [83, Proposition 2.3 and Corollaries 2.4 and 2.5] in the H¨older regularity setting;
see also [44, 35].

3.3. Rough integral. In this subsection, we go over the construction of rough integrals in
the current p-variation setting. Our goal is to construct an integral I(u) of u, whose increment
on [r, t] is given by Xt,r(u•) (see (3.21)), under the weaker assumption α + α0 ≤1 (in the
setting of Lemma 3.5). In this case, δΘ in (3.33) does not have sufficient regularity to apply
the sewing lemma (Lemma 3.4). In order to overcome this issue, we resort to rough path
theory, introduced in a seminal work by Lyons [109], and controlled paths, introduced by
Gubinelli [76]. Our presentation closely follows [76, Proposition 1] and [35, Subsection 4.3] in
the H¨older regularity setting.
We first recall the notion of rough paths and controlled paths.

4. Regularities of Young and rough drivers
One of the key steps in proving pathwise local well-posedness of SNLW (1.1) (at the
level of the vectorial integral formulation (1.29) for the interaction representation ⃗u defined
in (1.27)) is to make sense of the stochastic term ⃗Ψ(⃗u) = ⃗Ψβ(⃗u), formally defined in (1.30),
in the pathwise manner. For this purpose, our main goal in this section is to study almost
sure mapping properties of the random operators ⃗X and ⃗X, defined in (1.32) and (1.37),
respectively. See Propositions 4.1 and 4.2, where the random tensor estimate (Lemma 2.12)
plays a crucial role in estimating the operator norms of these random operators.

With this notation, we view the Young driver ⃗X in (4.3) (with 1
2 < β < 1) and the rough
driver (⃗X, ⃗X) (with β = 1
2) as random operators given by (multiple) stochastic integrals with
respect to (fractional) Brownian motions, as introduced in Subsection 2.4. We study their
almost sure mapping properties by applying the random tensor estimate (Lemma 2.12). In
Subsection 4.1, we consider the Young case, while we treat the rough case in Subsection 4.2.

5. Deterministic nonlinear estimates
In this section, we establish the deterministic nonlinear estimates to handle the nonlinear
Duhamel integral part of the Duhamel formulation (1.3) (more precisely, at the level of
the interaction representation in the vector-valued formulation; see (1.29)). The material
presented in this section is standard for those in dispersive PDEs, but we include some details,
in particular for readers in stochastic analysis who may not be familiar with the subject.
Moreover, our presentation in Subsection 5.2 (see Proposition 5.7), following [79, Subsection
3.1], provides a suitable wave admissible pair (q, r) (and a dual admissible pair (eq, er)) in a
concise manner, which is of interest in its own right.
In Subsection 5.1, we cover the one-dimensional case. In this case, the wave equation is non-
dispersive and thus we treat the nonlinearity simply via Sobolev’s embedding. In Subsection 5.2,
we go over the Strichartz estimate, following the presentation in [79, Subsection 3.1]. In
Subsection 5.3, we treat the case d ≥2 by employing the Strichartz estimates.
5.1. One-dimensional case. In this subsection, we consider the case d = 1. As mentioned
above, the one-dimensional wave equation is not dispersive and, as a consequence, there are no
Strichartz estimates. In this case, the critical regularity scrit is given by the Sobolev exponent
as in (1.20). See [45] for a further discussion.

5.2. Strichartz estimates. In order to establish nonlinear estimates in the higher dimensional
setting, we need to exploit dispersion in the form of the Strichartz estimates. In this subsection,
we recall the Strichartz estimates and also establish a key proposition (Proposition 5.7) which
guarantees existence of suitable s-admissible and dual s-admissible pairs in studying the
(deterministic) NLW (1.15) via the Strichartz estimates.

We now state the Strichartz estimates for the wave equation. The Strichartz estimates

of the YDE (6.1) follows from Banach’s fixed point theorem. Local Lipschitz continuity of
the solution map in (6.3) follows from a slight modification of [90, Part 3 of the proof of
Theorem 1.1 on p. 346] with (3.30) in Lemma 3.5.
□

Appendix A. Pathwise solutions coincide with Ito solutions
In this section, we focus on the rough case (= white-in-time case with β = 1
2) and show
that our pathwise solutions constructed in Theorem 1.5 agree with classical Ito solutions. In
Subsection A.1, we first go over the Ito solution theory for SNLW (1.1) on Td for readers’
convenience, as it does not seem to be written in the literature. We then compare our pathwise
solutions with Ito solutions in Subsection A.2.


## Page 76


76
A. CHAPOUTO, J. LI, AND T. OH
A.1. Review of the Ito solution theory. In this subsection, we briefly go over the Ito
solution theory for SNLW (1.1) on Td (more precisely, its Duhamel formulation (1.3)). Here,
the Ito solution theory refers to a solution theory where we interpret the stochastic convolution
Ψ(u) in (1.5) as an Ito integral.
Proposition A.1. Under the assumption of Theorem 1.5 (see in particular (1.21) and
(1.24)), given Φ ∈HS(L2(Td); Hσ(Td)), satisfying (1.22), SNLW (1.1) with a multiplicative
white-in-time noise is locally well-posed in Hs(Td) in the Ito sense.
Here, we stated Proposition A.1 for SNLW on Td but it also holds on Rd (with the same
proof). See also Remark A.3.
Proof of Proposition A.1. Proposition A.1 follows from a standard contraction argument in
L2
ad(Ω; Xs(T)) (see (A.1), (A.3), and (A.4) below) of processes adapted to the filtration
generated by W
1
2 in (1.6), using the truncation method as in [54, 55] for stochastic NLS with
multiplicative noises, and we only indicate necessary ingredients. Moreover, we restrict our
attention to d ≥2 in the following, since the necessary nonlinear estimate for d = 1 follows
more easily without using the Strichartz estimate (just by using (5.3)).
We first establish nonlinear estimates.

We now present a proof of Lemma A.6.
Proof of Lemma A.6. We follow the proof of [69, Proposition 5.1]. There are, however, several
differences:
(i) Unlike the scalar case considered in [69, Proposition 5.1], our integrator ⃗X is operator-
valued, which depends on the variable of integration, not just endpoints; see ⃗A1(Π) in
(A.36) and (A.35). See also (A.41).
(ii) In [69, Proposition 5.1], almost sure agreement such as (A.22) was shown for fixed
t ≥0, where a good set of full probability may depend on t. In order to prove (A.22),
we need to be able to find a good set of full probability which works for all 0 ≤t ≤τ.

Appendix D. On the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality
In this section, we recall the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality in a Banach space setting,
which we used in the proof of Lemma A.2. For this purpose, we first go over the basic
definition of γ-radonifying operators. These operators appear as natural extensions of Hilbert-
Schmidt operators to a Banach space setting. A practical example is the theory of stochastic
integrations in the Banach space setting, where γ-radonifying operators suitably generalize the
role played by Hilbert-Schmidt operators in the more ordinary Hilbert space setting [22, 165].
See [94, Chapter 9] for a detailed discussion on γ-radonifying operators.
Let H be a separable Hilbert space and B be a Banach space. An operator S ∈L(H; B) is
said to be of finite rank if it can be represented as
S(·) =

⟨· , en⟩xn,
where ⟨· , · ⟩denotes the inner product in H, {en}N
n=1 is orthonormal in H, and {xn}N
n=1 ⊂B.
Definition D.1. We define the space γ(H; B) as the closure of the set of finite rank operators
in L(H; B) under the norm:
∥S∥γ(H;B) = sup

where {en}n∈N is an orthonormal basis for H and {gn}n∈N is a sequence of independent
standard real-valued Gaussian random variables.
An operator S ∈γ(H; B) is called a
γ-radonifying operator.
The definition (D.1) is independent of a choice of orthonormal basis {en}n∈N for H. In
the following, it is understood that the results are independent of this choice. Note that
(γ(H; B), ∥· ∥γ(H;B)) is a Banach space and is separable whenever B is separable.
Remark D.2. The L2(Ω)-norm appearing in (D.1) can be replaced with Lp(Ω) for any
1 ≤p < ∞. Strictly speaking, this creates a family of spaces γp(H; B), however, by the
Kahane-Khintchine inequality (Theorem 6.2.6 in [94]), all these norms are equivalent. Hence,
it suffices to consider the most natural choice p = 2 and set γ(H; B) := γ2(H; B).
Lemma D.3 (Theorem 9.1.17 in [94]). An operator S ∈L(H; B) is γ-radonifying if and only
if the sum P

basis {en}n∈N for H. If S ∈γ(H; B), then this sum also converges almost surely and defines
a B-valued Gaussian random variable with covariance operator SS∗. Furthermore, we have
